# Render

Contributor reference for Haptic's main-thread Babylon renderer: Engine ownership, the frame path, resources, lighting, Play presentation, feature owners and the Babylon 9.29 workarounds that must survive upgrades.

## Ownership and boundaries

| Area | Owner |
| --- | --- |
| Every Babylon object (Engine, Scenes, materials, textures, FrameGraphs, particles, audio voices) | [`packages/render/src/`](../../../packages/render/src/) (`@haptic/render`) |
| Engine/session construction | [`create-engine.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/create-engine.ts) (`createEngine`, `createAppEngine`; handle and option types in `create-engine-types.ts`, editor viewport camera, gizmos, handles and gestures in `editor-viewport-tools.ts`, Play pointer, touch, wheel and key input in `play-game-input.ts`, per-slot outlines, deformers, fog volumes, reflection probes and area lights in `play-visual-bindings.ts`, e2e mesh readouts in `play-handle-inspection.ts`), [`apps/editor/src/lib/project-engine.ts`](../../../apps/editor/src/lib/project-engine.ts) (`createProjectEngineSession`), [`engine-session.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/engine-session.ts) (`createEngineSession`: one WebGL2 Engine on its own canvas) |
| Editor project Engine wiring: controller sync, Retry, shared Engine registration | `useProjectRendering` ([`apps/editor/src/context/use-project-rendering.ts`](../../../apps/editor/src/context/use-project-rendering.ts)), called by `PlayProvider`, over [`project-engine.ts`](../../../apps/editor/src/lib/project-engine.ts) and [`project-rendering-recovery.ts`](../../../apps/editor/src/lib/project-rendering-recovery.ts) |
| Color pipeline, scalability, shadow presets (Babylon-free) | [`packages/core/src/`](../../../packages/core/src/) (`render-effects.ts`, `scalability.ts`, `shadows.ts`, `render-setting-ownership.ts`) |
| Water/cable/wave math shared with the worker | [`packages/core/src/`](../../../packages/core/src/) (`water.ts`, `water-blend.ts`, `cable-simulation.ts`), [`packages/runtime/src/water-world.ts`](../../../packages/runtime/src/water-world.ts) |

- The game worker ([`packages/runtime/`](../../../packages/runtime/)) never imports Babylon. It sends render commands (`assignMesh`, `assignMaterial`, `assignParticle`, `debugDraw`, …) and snapshot buffers through [`packages/bridge/`](../../../packages/bridge/); render applies them.
- UI packages (`ui`, `editor-kit`, `graph-ui`) never import Babylon (ESLint `boundary/ui-packages`); they talk through the engine command bus.
- Babylon-free IR packages (`shader-graph`, `particle-graph`, `assets`) own schemas; render lowers or applies them. Never store Babylon serialization as an asset payload.
- Construct `Texture` only in [`resource-cache.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/resource-cache.ts) (ESLint `boundary/render-texture-cache`).
- The game HUD / UserInterface system is removed; Print HUD is DOM (`print-hud` in core). SceneLayers are the only in-game UI path ([scene-layers.md](scene-layers.md)).

## One Engine per project

- One project Engine on a hidden constructor canvas. **Never create a second WebGL context** (context caps). Scene viewport and Play bind visible canvases with `registerView(canvas, undefined, true)` and present as below; Material/Model/Animation/Skeleton/Particle/Prefab previews and thumbnails render to an RTT and blit (`createRttCanvasBlitter`, `flipReadPixelsRgba` in [`flip-read-pixels.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/flip-read-pixels.ts)). Editor code obtains it with `ensureSharedEngine`.
- Only exception: Preview Build's iframe (its own cold Engine). Do not add preview Engines.
- Previews must not `registerView` or `scene.render()` to the default framebuffer; Scene/Play own it. Do not move the Scene viewport to an RTT: Play blits from the Scene Engine framebuffer.
- A colour target the forward graph draws into with a depth texture (material previews and thumbnails, views presented through a target, the Scene Layers' shared target) comes from `createDepthTextureTarget` ([`depth-texture-target.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/depth-texture-target.ts)): the texture is its only depth (`generateDepthBuffer: false`), as Babylon's WebGL depth texture otherwise also allocates an unused depth renderbuffer of the target's size; the shadow atlas follows the same rule. Each layer draws into the Scene Layers' shared target directly through a view of its own Scene sharing its colour and depth textures, so layers composite in the target rather than one copy replacing another. The Scene Layer passes' output is colour only (no depth texture or renderbuffer): their graph draws through its chain's scene colour and depth, or, when no chain supplies them (a stack whose every entry dropped out), through the view's own pair, which `ForwardSceneFrameGraph` declares for any output target without depth, and copies colour into it.
- View presentation ([`direct-view-presentation.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/direct-view-presentation.ts), driven by [`registered-view-admission.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/registered-view-admission.ts)): while exactly one enabled registered view has a layout box (`viewCanvasHasLayoutBox`; a hidden dock tab's view stays enabled but is neither admitted nor counted), the Engine canvas itself is inserted as the view canvas's next sibling, placed over it (same box at device-pixel offsets, `pointer-events: none`, `aria-hidden`, and the view canvas's inline `image-rendering`, so a pixelated upscale stays pixelated) and shown, with no per-frame copy (on WebKit a copy is a CPU blit with a CPU/GPU sync). Input, focus, picking (`mapCanvasPointer`, GPU pick, CPU ray) and gizmos keep using the view canvas, whose bitmap keeps the presented size; overlays meant to sit above the image are positioned and come later in the document. Two or more such views, a view canvas in another document (a popout window), WebGL1, a multisampled default framebuffer, or `setDirectViewPresentationEnabled(false)` copy the Engine canvas into each view's 2D canvas instead; the departing view canvas, and a released one, first receive the held image, so they keep their last frame. Engine sessions created with `presentViewsDirectly` present directly (checked per frame through `Presentation.allowDirect`): the editor's project Engine (`createProjectEngineSession`) and the exported player's and Preview Build's (`startPlayerWithEngine`, whose Engine canvas, never otherwise in the page, covers `#game`). An Engine a view creates for itself copies. Test builds install `installDirectViewReadSync` in each page that reads view canvases (the editor's `main.tsx`, the player's `main.ts`, since a Preview Build iframe is its own realm), so `drawImage`, `getImageData`, `toDataURL`, `toBlob` and `createImageBitmap` of a covered view canvas see the shown image. A covered view's steady frame measures the view canvas's box (no event reports it moving while keeping its size: a sibling above it growing, a margin or inline style changing), writes the Engine canvas's style only when the placement changed, keeps the read sync it was covered with, and never takes the view canvas's 2D context, which only a copy or a held-image handover writes. While a view presents directly, `renderDiagnostics().presentation` reports `direct: true` and `copyMs` is the held-image snapshot and placement, not a canvas copy. A context loss hides the Engine canvas without a held image to hand over, so the view canvas (blank after direct frames) shows until the restored Engine presents again.
- Z-order: the placed Engine canvas is the view canvas's next sibling, positioned absolutely with no `z-index`. An absolutely positioned overlay that comes before the view canvas in the DOM and has no `z-index` now paints under the Engine canvas; give viewport overlays a `z-index` or place them after the view canvas.
- Direct held frames: each coherent frame blits the default framebuffer into the view's held renderbuffer, also with `preserveDrawingBuffer`: the next frame's draw overwrites the default framebuffer before it is known to be coherent (the world's graph writes its output, then the layers draw over it), and an incoherent frame must put the last coherent image back, so the copy cannot wait for the transitions; an incoherent frame, a view step that resized the buffer, or an `engine.setSize` outside a view step, blits it back before the browser presents. A step whose loop declined to draw (`Presentation.dirty`, render on demand) blits nothing: the default framebuffer is preserved. A view that stops being covered (another sole view, the copy fallback, release) gets its held image in its own canvas once, so it keeps its last frame. Releasing a view untracks its canvas, and parking a disposed Engine's canvas removes it instead of returning it to the document. While a view is covered, nothing may draw to the default framebuffer outside that view's step. Test builds write the presented image into a covered view canvas before `drawImage`, `getImageData`, `toDataURL`, `toBlob` or `createImageBitmap` read it (`installDirectViewReadSync`).
- The project Engine keeps `preserveDrawingBuffer: true` unless the `preservebuffer off` debug command stored `haptic:debug:preserveDrawingBuffer` (`preserveDrawingBufferSetting`, read when the project Engine is created, so it applies after an editor reload or when the project is reopened). Off is a presentation-cost comparison for the Feature Test Check's Breakdown on a device, which switches it itself across one reload for its A/B, never a shipped mode: the Engine's own reads (a view copy, the held-image blit) happen in the task that drew the frame, and GPU picking, Render Target Captures and thumbnails read their own render targets (their e2e pass with it off), but in a frame the Engine skips (the frame cap, a held frame) the Engine canvas reads back blank, so `e2e/viewport-direct-presentation.spec.ts`, whose probe samples every animation frame, fails with it off. The Breakdown fingerprint reports the live attribute as `presentation.preserve`. GPU-busy timing (`gpubusy`, [`gpu-busy-probe.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/gpu-busy-probe.ts)) installs one probe per Engine on first use: it submits a fence at `onEndFrameObservable` and polls its fences once at `onBeginFrameObservable` (no timer task of its own, so a completion is read at frame granularity); off, it does nothing per frame.
- **GPU backpressure** ([`frame-backpressure.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/frame-backpressure.ts)): one gate per Engine caps rendered frames queued on the GPU (`framesinflight`, default 2, `off` or 3). A frame that renders submits a marker at the Engine's frame end (a WebGL2 fence, the same `FrameMarker` code as `gpubusy`); `admit()` runs after `shouldRenderFrame` in the render loop and in registered-view admission, polls markers with a zero timeout (never blocks or awaits), and skips the render while `mode` are unfinished, once per Engine frame in its count. The loop polls at most once per Engine frame, as each poll (`clientWaitSync`) can be a synchronous round trip to WebKit's GPU process: `admit` only while the queue is at its limit, and until it is below it; otherwise the frame end, before it submits its marker, retires what finished. `stats()` polls on every call. A loading owner's presentation frame (`hasLoadingFrame`, `admit(true)`) renders past the limit and still counts in flight: it already waits for its own GPU completion, one attempt at a time, under its own budget, and holding it behind queued frames would charge their GPU time (software GL compiling programs at first draw) to its first-frame deadline and fail the load. A skipped frame changes nothing on screen (no draw, no view copy); snapshots, simulation and input go on. Without it, a slow GPU (software GL) queued seconds of frames, and every page task, read-back and context teardown (closing the Preview Build's iframe) waited behind them. `RenderDiagnostics.frameBackpressure` reports mode, frames in flight and skips (Stats' `stat draws` row, Feature Test samples' `framesInFlight`). NullEngine has no markers, so it never skips.
- Held images are a fixed presentation cost outside the managed ledger, like the Engine's own framebuffer: one RGBA8 renderbuffer per held view (4 bytes per pixel, about 22 MB at 2732×2048). They exist while a view is presented directly and go when presentation copies or the view is released.
- View canvases take their 2D context with `VIEW_CANVAS_2D_ATTRIBUTES` (`willReadFrequently: false`, [`registered-view-admission.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/registered-view-admission.ts)), and any 2D canvas the page reads back (`getImageData`, e.g. glyph rasterization in [`text2d-bitmap.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/text2d-bitmap.ts)) declares `willReadFrequently: true`. After readbacks on default 2D canvases, Chromium creates later default contexts unaccelerated, and each view copy then becomes a synchronous GPU readback.
- Closing a Scene tab disposes its Scene and cache retains, never the Engine. Closing the project disposes the Engine.
- Shared views size the framebuffer with `engine.setSize` from the view's CSS box, never `engine.resize()` (it follows the hidden constructor canvas). Resize only on integer CSS size change; ignore zero sizes. A view whose canvas has no layout box (a hidden dock tab) is not admitted: it neither sizes the Engine (that would collapse it to 1×1) nor draws, because a 1×1 frame turns the water scene copy off, re-plans the graph and compiles the variants it then draws.
- Every Engine is WebGL2 (`createEngineSession` rejects a WebGL1 context); there is no backend setting. Uncertain cleanup blocks any new allocation (never publish a partially released context).
- A failed project Engine offers Retry ([`project-engine.ts`](../../../apps/editor/src/lib/project-engine.ts), [`project-rendering-recovery.ts`](../../../apps/editor/src/lib/project-rendering-recovery.ts)): Preview Build has its own Engine, so Retry asks the user to stop it; a game on the project Engine stops first, and Retry waits, bounded, for its release, reports a quarantined or still-releasing game instead of allocating, then creates a new Engine. Babylon restores a lost WebGL2 context in place: a lost WebGL2 Engine stays usable, and its views reload on `onContextRestoredObservable`. A preparation running while the context is lost fails (Babylon cannot create textures) and latches its graph's failure, which holds every later frame; so the restore restarts the world's and every SceneLayer's preparation (`SceneRenderCoordinator.invalidate`, `SceneLayerCompositor.invalidate`) in `createEngine`.
- **Large World Rendering** (`RenderProjectSettings.largeWorldRendering`, off by default; [`large-world.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/large-world.ts)) is Babylon's floating origin (`useLargeWorldRendering`): the origin is the camera's eye, so every world matrix a draw uploads, and every instance row's translation, is the placement minus the eye in 64 bits, then 32 (`OffsetWorldToRef`, `scene.floatingOriginOffset`). It keeps geometry kilometres out steady; off, an uploaded placement does not change when the camera moves (retained shadow caster rows and Scene Layer draw batch rows, which follow `floatingOriginOffset`, keep their work), and each draw skips the offset matrices. Babylon fixes it per Scene at construction from the Engine's options, so it is the Engine's for its life: `createAppEngine` / `createEngineSession` take it, the editor's project Engine controller keys on project and mode and replaces the Engine when the setting changes (as a project switch or Retry does; a running game keeps its Engine until Stop), shared-Engine and overlay Play, previews and thumbnails follow the project Engine, and the player and Preview Build build theirs from the exported manifest. Code that places things relative to the eye reads `scene.floatingOriginOffset` (zero with the mode off) or checks `scene.floatingOriginMode`, never assumes either mode.
- Matrix precision is page-global in Babylon: every Engine constructor, a `NullEngine`'s included, sets the type later matrices are built with, and only the page's first converts existing ones (Babylon's own temporaries, the floating origin's among them). Every Engine the page builds therefore passes `useHighPrecisionMatrix` (`PAGE_MATRIX_PRECISION`, `largeWorldEngineOptions`, `createHeadlessEngine` for model conversion and tests, the Havok backend's own option), whatever the mode: a 32-bit Engine or conversion before a large-world Engine would leave positions rounded before the eye is subtracted.
- Every shader source is GLSL: NodeMaterials, Haptic's custom NodeMaterial blocks and built-in helpers (grid, camera bounds).
- The editor grid is one shader plane under the 3D eye (2D: the camera target), snapped to the major spacing. Each level (minor, major, ×10 steps) fades in per fragment from its on-screen cell size, so zoom, distance and grazing angles blend without a global fade; the plane's distance fade scales with eye height and stays inside the far clip ([`editor-grid.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/editor-grid.ts)). Every fragment pays for every level, and software GL (SwiftShader, as in CI) pays for each instruction while early-outs and loops cost it more than they skip, so keep per-level work minimal and straight-line: one `log` per fragment, levels composite three premultiplied sums (alpha, emphasis-weighted alpha, alpha along v) and colour resolves once, and level line widths stay at or under one pixel.
- Context MSAA off; `adaptToDeviceRatio: false`, resolution via `setHardwareScalingLevel` ([`hardware-scaling.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/hardware-scaling.ts)). `HardwareScalingController` holds a static level: Engine Settings scaling is the initial level, `configureQuality` sets 1 / Resolution Scale and `setSettingsLevel` the Engine Settings level (capped at 4); the last call wins and nothing changes it at runtime, so a context restore keeps it. No dynamic resolution: each level change resizes the output and rebuilds the view's frame graph. A Scene given no project quality (an editor view or asset preview without the project's settings) resolves Resolution Scale 1 unless an override sets one (`resolveSceneRenderingQuality`): it has no tier, so it never takes the default tier's scale.

## Frame path

- [`framegraph-forward-scene.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/framegraph-forward-scene.ts) (`ForwardSceneFrameGraph`) is the **only** scene draw path; there is no native `Scene.render` fallback. Every Play world, SceneLayer, editor viewport, preview and thumbnail owns a `SceneRenderCoordinator` ([`scene-render-coordinator.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/scene-render-coordinator.ts)). Native shader warm precedes graph preparation; preparation builds and probes, never draws.
- Task order (`taskNames()`): `Forward admitted shadows` → `Forward cull` → geometry tasks when a chain needs them → `Screen shadows` when planned → `Forward objects` → authored post-process, settings effects, shared outlines → `Scene post-process output` (only when a chain exists; then it is the view's only output writer; an auxiliary view without a chain writes its own scene targets in `Capture output`). A settings chain whose last own stage is one of its post processes drawing the output's size (Display Color, bloom's merge, FXAA or FSR's sharpen; `SceneEffectsGraph.outputWriter`, `rendersToOutput`) has that stage render into the output instead, with no copy after it: one full-screen pass and one full-size intermediate fewer (half-float for a Scene Linear Display Color). The stage's depth test is off, since Babylon's post processes test depth by default and the backbuffer may hold another pass's depth. Shared outlines over a chain (`SceneEffectsGraphOptions.outlines`) land on the image before antialiasing. One rule: a chain with a Display Color pass (bloom or image processing; always the stage right before the outlines) has that pass composite the stroke as its last stage, and the outline task, created composed externally, draws only its masks before it (no composition pass, so one full-screen pass fewer for every Anti-Aliasing setting); a chain without one (FXAA alone, the spatial effects alone) keeps the outlines' own composition, blended in place onto the image before it: with neither FXAA nor FSR after them, the stage before them renders into the output and they blend onto it there (`ForwardSceneFrameGraph` adds no copy); with FXAA or FSR they blend onto that stage's own target, which FXAA or the upscale reads. A chain ending in the spatial effects, or in outlines over them, or drawn at a scale of the output without FSR, keeps the copy. Water scene copy splits the object pass ([Water](#water)).
- The view's clear (the Scene's clear colour, linear under Scene Linear, and `autoClear` / `autoClearDepthAndStencil`) runs at the start of `Forward objects`, on its own target (`ManagedShadowObjectRendererTask.startClear`, read each frame from the view's `Forward clear` task, which is then not in the graph): nothing before that pass reads or draws the scene targets, and a clear pass of its own would make a tile-based GPU store the cleared, multisampled targets for the objects pass to load again. Only the water split's own-pair form keeps `Forward clear` as a task (`Forward cull` → `Water split` → `Forward clear`), since the split switches it per frame. No other pass (`Forward transparent`, `Water opaque`) clears.
- The spatial geometry pass (`LogicalGeometryTask`, [`framegraph-logical-buffers.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/framegraph-logical-buffers.ts)) prepares once on its own target: Babylon's clear for every other clear type, then the view depth at the camera's far plane (Babylon 9.29 clears MaxViewZ to zero) in one clear with its depth (`clearsDepth`), or the seed; its target stays bound for the draws. After them it discards its depth (`discardsDepth`, `invalidateFramebuffer` while the target is bound): nothing reads `Spatial Geometry Z` after the pass, and every frame clears or seeds it again. A pass without the view depth (the GPU picker's, an authored chain's) prepares as Babylon does. `e2e/main-pass-exactness.spec.ts` holds the frames and captures byte-identical to the separate clear pass and the earlier geometry preparation, and checks each run's pass structure (`GlSignatureRecorder`).
- The spatial geometry pass draws the cull's world list (`CameraOutputCullTask.worldObjectList`): the culled meshes less editor and Play helper visuals and meshes drawing a Text or User Interface Material, whose fragment output writes colour only. Drawn into its multi-target framebuffer, such a mesh leaves active draw buffers without a fragment output, an `INVALID_OPERATION` on ANGLE that fails the view's presentation. Neither Play's layer Scenes nor a Scene Layer's editor view (`excludeSceneSpatialEffects`) plans spatial effects; the filter guards any other view that draws such a mesh.
- Full-screen passes start from a clear ([`full-target-clear.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/full-target-clear.ts)): after each build `startFullScreenPassesWithClear` wraps the enabled render passes of every task whose passes are each one full-screen draw (`fullTargetCover`: any `FrameGraphPostProcessTask`, Display Color included, the bloom blur's three stages, the output copies `FrameGraphCopyToBackbufferColorTask` and `FrameGraphCopyToTextureTask`, and Haptic tasks with `coversOutput`: authored post-processes and the water scene copy), so each clears its colour target at its full viewport before its draw and a tile-based GPU (ANGLE on Metal) starts the pass from a clear instead of a load. Only in frames whose draw writes every pixel and channel: effect ready (an unready full-screen draw draws nothing), blending off, colour writes on, no stencil state, no viewport, no depth test against a depth buffer (a stage drawing into the backbuffer has its test off), and no `discard` in the processed fragment source (`fragmentDiscards`; a Custom GLSL node can discard). A clear, never an invalidate: a pass that did not cover its target would change the picture on every platform, where the main pass proof sees it. Left alone: disabled passes (a skipped stage's copy, idle empty passes), the outlines' own composition (blends; the stage before it, into the same target, covers it; a Display Color pass that composites the stroke is an ordinary covering post process), depth seeds (colour writes off), the multisample present (a blit), and the display conversion of Fast captures and reflection probes (`display-conversion.ts`, outside the graphs). `fullTargetClear.clear` is a member so the proof's reference run restores the load. On the backbuffer, in a Play frame whose Scene Layers draw straight over the world with `layerdebug skipclears` on (`foldsBackbufferDepthStencil`, set by `SceneLayerCompositor.beforeWorld`), the clear also clears depth and stencil (depth writes on for it, then put back): a full-screen pass neither tests nor writes them, and the first layer's own depth-stencil clear then finds them cleared and is left out ([`backbuffer-depth-tracker.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/backbuffer-depth-tracker.ts), [scene-layers.md](scene-layers.md#runtime-and-render)).
- Draws per pass ([`draw-passes.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/draw-passes.ts), `RenderDiagnostics.drawPasses`): each `createEngine` view attributes its frame's draw calls between `beginEngineDrawCallFrame` and the frame's end to `objects`, `geometry`, `shadows`, `captures`, `mirrors`, `probes`, `water`, `outlines`, `post`, `layers`, `cull`, `readiness`, `particles`, `observers` and `other` (the last five exist for the time split and normally draw nothing); the last drawn frame's split adds up to its `drawCalls`, a held frame keeps the previous one. Counted with Babylon's own counter (`_drawCalls`, which `AbstractEngine._reportDrawCall` adds to from every `drawElementsType`/`drawArraysType`; NullEngine's draws report nothing, so tests report them themselves), read where a pass starts and ends: `observeTaskDrawPass` adds a before and an after observer, both first in their observable (so the task's own before-observers, such as the shared outline's sync, are the task's, and the capture views the shadows task's after-observer draws are not), to every task `ForwardSceneFrameGraph` builds, classified by identity (`Forward cull`; `Forward admitted shadows`; geometry tasks and `Screen shadows` (also as `Half-resolution marches`, with the opt-in merged marches); `Shared outlines` (its masks and its own composition; a stroke composited by the Display Color pass is that pass's draw, so it counts to `post`); post-process and settings-effect stages and a chain's output copy; object renderers, `Forward transparent` and `Water opaque` included; the water split's own tasks; the rest `other`). Out-of-graph owners open an owner pass that keeps what nests in it (a capture view's own sun maps and post-processing count to `captures`): `RenderTargetCaptures.draw` (Fast and view captures), reflection probe captures, Mirror and water planar reflection passes, the water FFT dispatch, and the Scene Layer compositor in `create-engine.ts`. A draw counts to the innermost pass running, so nothing counts twice; outside a view's frame (previews, thumbnails, loading work) passes are no-ops. One reused record per view and a counter read per pass boundary: it stays on. The view's main-thread time is attributed the same way (`RenderDiagnostics.drawPassMs`, ms by pass, adding up to the frame's begin-to-end span inside `cpuMs`): off until the view's diagnostics are first read (`DrawPassFrame.timePasses`), then one `performance.now()` per pass boundary from the next frame on; a frame is timed whole or not at all. The time split also brackets work no graph task owns, so `other` is the true remainder (clears, animations, world matrices, flushes before the probes, the frame graph's own overhead): `cull` is `Forward cull` whole (`CameraOutputCullTask._execute`, the occlusion filter included, which a task observer would end before; the task's own observer pass is `cull` too, so Babylon's frustum test inside it is never `other`); `readiness` is `ForwardSceneFrameGraph.readiness`, `sceneStrictlyReady`, the pre-draw part of `render` (`admitFrame`: membership sync, shadow admission, restale, probes) and the post-draw membership sync; `observers` runs from `pinnedGraphRender` (before Scene.render's camera updates) to `onBeforeRenderTargetsRenderObservable`, so every `onBeforeRenderObservable` observer is in it except what opens an owner pass (Mirror, capture and probe updates); `particles` is each system's own `animate` (`timeParticleAnimation` wraps it once per system at that same observable, because Babylon animates particles inside the frame graph render with no observable around them). The bracketing allocates nothing per frame; `particles` costs a pass boundary pair per started system.
- Unsupported, so preparation rejects and nothing draws: frozen active-mesh queues, camera post-processes, rig/multiple cameras, multisample/cube/array or caller-bound targets, custom render functions, unmanaged shadow generators. Editor scenes therefore freeze world matrices and materials but never the active-mesh queue.
- Strict readiness is probed only after invalidation, never per steady frame, except while a variant swap is pending (below). Anything that changes shader-relevant state outside Scene observables must call `markSceneReadinessDirty` ([`scene-readiness-signal.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/scene-readiness-signal.ts)). Readiness probes restore camera, matrices, UBO, viewport and render pass even when callbacks throw. An invalidated frame probes once: `sceneStrictlyReady` admits lights and shadows before it probes, so what that admission stales (a mesh the shadow controller first sees, a light capacity that changed) is covered by the probe instead of staling it again for `readiness`, and a frame whose probe fails is admitted without a second one (`readiness(camera, false)`), since it is not drawn either way and the next frame asks again.
- What does not stale readiness, because it leaves nothing new to compile: a mesh that leaves the Scene (the mesh count falls by exactly the removals Babylon announced, which it does at once; a count that fell by fewer holds an addition and marks), except an instance, whose source's batch can draw with another variant once it is gone (its last one, so it draws without instancing again; a pool group's warm-up stand-in); a particle system that leaves (Babylon takes a disposed one out of the Scene without announcing it, so a changed count marks only when it holds a system not seen before); a particle system's readiness placeholder texture joining or leaving ([`particle-readiness-texture.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/particle-readiness-texture.ts): no shader samples it, and it is left out of the texture count); a mesh the shadow controller synced that came out receiving and casting as it went in (a particle emitter's anchor). Babylon announces an added mesh on a later task: a frame drawn before then finds it by the mesh count and marks it, and the announcement marks nothing more. Other removals (a Material, a texture, a light, a camera) still mark.
- Every mark carries a reason (`SceneReadinessDirtyReason`, a fixed code; `"other"` when a caller gives none), for diagnostics only. Once `diagnostics()` was read, `ForwardSceneFrameGraph` counts each strict probe once for every reason marked since the last passing probe, a probe with none that only rechecks a pending variant swap (`recheckVariantSwaps`) as a swap recheck, and probes that fail (`rendererWork.readinessProbeReasons`, `readinessSwapRechecks`, `failedReadinessProbes`). A measured probe that fails also leaves what it found unready, in the order it met them and at most `MAX_READINESS_BLOCKERS` (`rendererWork.readinessBlockers`, [`readiness-blockers.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/readiness-blockers.ts)): a mesh and its Material with where each Effect it requested stands (processing its sources, linking, failed), the first unready graph task, a texture, a particle system, a render target. Each probe site names its blocker through `noteReadinessBlocker`, which describes nothing unless a measured probe is running; a new reason for a probe to fail names itself the same way. Unmeasured, a mark costs one branch.
- A mark may name the only meshes whose variants its change can have touched (`markSceneReadinessDirty(scene, reason, scope)`; an empty scope for a change no mesh draws with). Scoped: a mesh that joined the Scene, the source an instance joined or left, the meshes a shadow sync changed, a particle system's Material bind and its end, and a changed count of particle systems or custom readiness checks. Every other mark can have touched any mesh. A world view's steady frame whose marks since its last passing probe were all scoped probes only those meshes (`readiness-scope.ts`): each per-mesh check answers ready for any other mesh, an instance counts with its source, and a render target asks only the scope's meshes of its list once a whole probe has found it ready (a mirror or shadow map made since asks them all). Everything a probe checks that is not per mesh still runs whole. A render target that builds its list per pass is told which meshes it is asked (`readinessProbeListScope`): a shadow map then lists only those casters and the instances they draw (`ShadowCasterList.queryPlanesOf`), not every caster of every layer. The probe asks every mesh instead while a variant swap is pending (a hidden mesh outside the scope would be revealed by no round), when it tolerates no swap (a load, a preparation, a capture) and when it made a mark of its own for any mesh. A view that draws every frame and never tolerates a swap can ask for the same limit on its strict probes (`scopedStrictProbes`: a Scene Layer's renderer, so a visual that joins or starts to blend is probed for itself, not with the layer's every visual); its preparation and any mark that names no mesh still ask all. A probe passes over a mesh no pass draws (disabled, invisible or without geometry), so the mark that named it can be consumed before it draws: each view follows which meshes are undrawn through the render change journal's enabled, visibility and geometry writes (`openRenderChangeReader`, read before every probe and at each draw's end; meshes the journal does not hear are polled while undrawn), and a mesh that becomes drawable after a passing probe passed over it (a whole one, or a scoped one that named it) marks itself, scoped to it (`drawable`; `followDrawable`). So does a mesh whose `visibility` crosses 1 (`meshVisibility`): Babylon blends a mesh below it, so the crossing changes its variant (a glyph revealed from 0, a fade that ends). New vertex data with its indices (a redrawn text's `applyToMesh`) rebuilds a mesh's sub-meshes, whose draw wrappers hold no variant for any pass, the camera pass a probe asks included: with other vertex attribute kinds the mesh marks itself (`meshGeometry`), with the same ones it joins the next probe's scope without forcing one, so a text redrawn each frame probes nothing. Vertex updates in place keep the sub-meshes. Only meshes the view knows are followed: a draw batch's slot mesh is in no probe's list. A mesh hidden and shown again with no passing probe in between marks nothing, so a steady toggle or geometry update probes nothing; a reader that fell behind marks every mesh. `RenderDiagnostics.layerWork` lists each layer's own counters (`SceneLayerCompositor.diagnostics`), with how its draw batcher kept its work (`batching`, [Draw batching](#draw-batching)). `sceneReadinessRevision` counts what staled the readiness of a Scene's meshes (the marks that name a mesh or none in particular, and what its own view's graph hears from Babylon), for owners outside the graph: a Mirror's or planar water's reflection pass, which redraws until one draw left no mesh out, asks its target for that only for a mesh new to the pass or after the count moved ([`reflection-pass-readiness.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/reflection-pass-readiness.ts)), not after every draw. `rendererWork.scopedReadinessProbes` counts the scoped ones. A scoped probe that made a mark for any mesh itself counts as failed. `verifyScopedReadinessProbes(true)` is a debugging aid (test builds: `__hapticPlayTest.verifyScopedReadinessProbes`): each scoped probe that passes is followed by a probe of every mesh, which must pass, add no Effect to the Engine and, unless the scoped probe found a swap pending, ask it for none it already holds (a mesh outside the scope whose variant changed); a request that returns the Effect the asking mesh's only sub-mesh already draws with in that pass changed no variant (a dirty flag the scoped probe left unread, as a scrolled texture matrix sets) and counts as idle instead (`rendererWork.scopedReadinessIdleRequests`; one that names no mesh, a mesh with several sub-meshes or an Effect kept on its Material still disagrees); `rendererWork.scopedReadinessMismatches` counts those that do not, and `scopedReadinessMismatchCauses` says why the first eight did not: whether the probe of every mesh failed, compiled an Effect or asked for one, the reasons marked for the scoped probe, how many meshes it was limited to, and the first Effects asked for (the mesh whose check asked and its Material, the shader name, ready or pending, the start of its defines).
- A frame whose readiness changes while it draws is not rendered (`ForwardSceneFrameGraph.render` compares the readiness revision). The window ends as the graph's draw returns (`endDraw`), so it covers `Scene.render`'s before-render observers (captures, mirrors), animation and particle steps and the graph itself; `endDraw` first compares membership counts, since Babylon announces an added mesh or Material only after the frame. A change in `Scene.render`'s after-render observers (a Spawn Emitter creating or disposing its Emitters, [`particle-service.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/particle-service.ts)) cannot alter the drawn image, so the frame stays rendered; it still marks readiness dirty, so the next frame probes before it draws, and a validated presentation's post-draw probe sees it.
- Probes never replace a drawn Effect ([`isMeshFrameReady`](../../../packages/render/src/scene-perf.ts)): Babylon's hot swap (`allowShaderHotSwapping`, on by default) keeps a submesh drawing its previous, ready Effect while a newly requested variant compiles, and leaves its defines unprocessed, which is how a probe tells a swap. Replacing it would leave Babylon's frozen and same-frame readiness caches answering for an Effect still compiling. Inside `probeVariantSwaps(true, …)` ([`variant-swaps.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/variant-swaps.ts)) a swapping mesh is ready, drawing its previous Effect (the light block binds lights as data, so that Effect never binds a disposed light); everywhere else it waits for its new variant. A swap whose new variant failed to link, every fallback included (none of the Effects the mesh requested is still compiling and one has `getCompilationError()` with `allFallbacksProcessed()`), is over: Babylon draws the previous Effect for good, so the mesh is ready in every probe and the failure is logged once per Effect.
- Probes compile ahead on a parallel-compile driver (`KHR_parallel_shader_compile`; SwiftShader has none, so each request on it links inside the probe that makes it). iPad WebKit WebGL2 exposes it and its new effects stay pending while they link, so the limit below throttles there as on D3D11; its heavy programs finish roughly one after another, and the first draw of each new program still blocks the thread (about 100 ms each in raw WebGL), which no readiness probe sees. Babylon's scans stop at the first unready mesh (ObjectRenderer) and task (`FrameGraph.isReady`), so a pass's variants would link one after another, and each pass only once every earlier pass is ready. While fewer than `COMPILE_AHEAD_LIMIT` effects are pending on the Engine ([`driver-compile.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/driver-compile.ts)), the forward graph also probes the tasks after its first unready one, and the object, transparent and geometry passes and the GPU pick pass the meshes after theirs (`requestCompilesAhead`), including the depth pre-pass that an unready colour variant holds back. The limit counts every compile pending on the Engine (camera-pass probes and previews included) and the probe that crosses it may start several. Each mesh is probed ahead once until its pass is ready, the forward graph's readiness is invalidated (`markReadinessDirty`) or the pick pass drops its objects; the results are discarded, so the in-order scans alone decide readiness.
- Mask, geometry and pick passes are light-neutral ([`light-neutral-tasks.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/light-neutral-tasks.ts)): never toggle `light.shadowEnabled` around a pass; each toggle dirties every material's light defines.
- Blended materials with `needDepthPrePass` draw their depth pre-pass under their own render pass id ([`transparent-depth-pre-pass.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/transparent-depth-pre-pass.ts)); sharing one draw wrapper made frozen materials draw the depth variant as colour.
- Lean preparation ([`lean-render-preparation.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/lean-render-preparation.ts)): a game's world view (`ForwardSceneFrameGraphOptions.leanPreparation`, from `playMode`; never the editor viewport, a Scene Layer or an auxiliary view) replaces `_prepareRenderingManager` on its `Forward objects` renderer (the water split's opaque pass draws through it) and on each `LogicalGeometryTask` renderer of its graph; shadow maps, outline masks, Mirrors, captures and the water's transparent pass keep Babylon's. It queues exactly what Babylon's function queues, in the same order, with the per-mesh answers that almost never change kept per (renderer, mesh). What is kept and what invalidates it:
  - The LOD, only where fixed: no `customLODSelector` or automatic LOD's (`isAutoLodSelector`; every master has Babylon levels), the stock `getLOD`, and no `_LODLevels` on the mesh or an instance's source. The answer is the mesh or the instance's source, whose `_currentLOD` is written every frame. Invalidated by `RenderChange.Dispatch` (`addLODLevel`, `removeLODLevel`) and a replaced selector (compared every pass; refreshes every entry). A mesh with levels selects every frame through Babylon's per-frame memo.
  - `isEnabled() && isVisible`: `RenderChange.Enabled` and `RenderChange.Visibility` (the journal names descendants itself).
  - For the mesh the sub-meshes draw through (the LOD, or an instance acting as a regular mesh): each sub-mesh's root Material (`RenderChange.Material`: `_setMaterial`, `setMaterialForRenderPass`, a source's write naming its instances); its rendering group id and whether it is below full visibility or has vertex alpha (`RenderChange.Dispatch`: `renderingGroupId`, `hasVertexAlpha`; `RenderChange.Visibility`).
  - `RenderChange.Other` also refreshes the mesh; a journal overflow refreshes every entry. The reader leaves out Transform, Geometry and Style, which decide none of these.
- Read every pass instead of kept: readiness from the renderer's `customIsReadyFunction` where it has one (the geometry pass's), else from the fields `Mesh.isReady(false)` reads (`delayLoadState`, `_nodeDataStorage._isReady`; an instance's source's; any other `isReady` is called); the layer mask against the camera's; the sub-mesh array, its length and each element (a difference rebuilds the mesh's sub-mesh answers); a MultiMaterial or missing root through `SubMesh.getMaterial()` (its slot side effect and the default material); each group's `disableDepthPrePass`. Each distinct Material is asked once per pass whether its `needAlphaBlendingForMesh` and `needAlphaTestingForMesh` are Material's own, their answers for the four visibility and vertex-alpha variants (stand-in meshes) and `needDepthPrePass`; a Material that replaces either (OpenPBR, `overlay-visual-style.ts`) is asked per sub-mesh. Everything else with a reader still runs every frame as Babylon runs it: `isBlocked`, the billboard LOD's world matrix, `_preActivateForIntermediateRendering`, the active-mesh push, `_wasActiveLastFrame`, `_activate` (instance registration), the instance and intermediate flags, `_prepareSkeleton`, `_wasDispatched`, the queue pushes, `_renderingGroup`, the edges check, `_empty`, `_postActivate`, the bounding-box reset and boxes (the graph's tasks keep `enableBoundingBoxRendering` on) and particle dispatch.
- A mesh whose kept answers the journal cannot follow (`journalFollows` false for it, an ancestor or an instance's source), whose class replaces `isEnabled`, or whose LOD is not fixed takes Babylon's loop body for that mesh. A pass with a custom render list, `winterIsComing`, no render list, frozen meshes, several render pass ids, `maintainStateBetweenFrames` or no LOD camera runs Babylon's whole function. Each pass recomputes the kept entries at one list position in 32 (rotating) and heals any that differ (`rendererWork.leanPreparation.heals`, `healedFields`, which must stay empty). `verifyLeanPreparation` (`HAPTIC_PERF_VERIFY_PREPARATION`) has each pass run Babylon's preparation, which draws, then the lean one into a scratch `RenderingManager` with the activation state it writes saved and restored and instance registration suppressed, and compares the queues and active meshes element by element (`mismatches`, which must stay 0; a mismatch refreshes every entry).
- Presentation: the visible canvas changes only after a coherent draw; rebuilds, resizes and skipped frames hold the last image. First frame waits for GPU submission (an owned fence polled without blocking; never read GL errors before it signals) and the canvas copy.
- Held frames by reason (`RenderDiagnostics.presentation.heldReasons`, [`held-frames.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/held-frames.ts)): only the world draw makes a frame incoherent, so each `presentationStats.held` counts under the world result's `reason` (`SceneRenderCoordinator.render`: "Scene is not ready to render." for a failed strict scene probe (or no camera, a disposed view), or the FrameGraph's own: "FrameGraph preparation is required.", "FrameGraph is not ready.", a paused-redraw issue, an unsupported scene or a latched failure) or a `HELD_FRAME_LABELS` label: readiness changed before the graph's draw ended (not rendered, no reason; above), a frame report's capture found the frame not ready for presentation, or the world's pending presentation was not ready on a loaded view. A Scene Layer owner that is not ready never holds the frame: the compositor draws that layer's last output (`blitFallback`). Counting allocates only a reason's first key; at most `MAX_HELD_FRAME_REASONS` (8) keys, later reasons under "other". `renderDiagnostics()` returns a copy. The Feature Test Check prints them on the Play line and per Profile row.
- Variant swaps ([`scene-render-coordinator.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/scene-render-coordinator.ts)): after the first frame, a change to an existing Material's variant never holds the picture or pauses the game. A world view's steady frames (`compileHolds`, not auxiliary, `render(false)`, on a world Scene: `sceneBindsPreviousEffects`, which `createEngine` sets with `setSceneBindsPreviousEffects` for every Scene neither presented through an RTT nor given preview studio lights) probe inside `probeVariantSwaps(true, …)`. A Material whose variant changes therefore draws its previous variant a few frames longer instead of holding the last image. Play, the exported player, Preview Build and the editor's world view share this path.
  - Tolerance accepts every hot swap, not only light changes: any define change on an existing Material (a texture arriving) draws the old variant until the new one is ready, which is Babylon's own default (`allowShaderHotSwapping`), reading the current light block.
  - While a swap is pending each steady frame probes again (`recheckVariantSwaps`), so a Material adopts its new variant on the first frame that variant is ready, frozen ones included (the editor freezes library Materials, and a frozen NodeMaterial that swapped answers ready without asking again). A swap whose variant failed to link stops this re-probe.
  - A preparation nothing awaits (`requestPreparation`, whether a steady frame or a strict readiness question asked for it, such as Play's end-of-frame check, while a scalability transaction awaits its frame, finding a new output size first) probes as the view's steady frames do, since they are what draws once it is done; a strict question asks again afterwards. Until the view has drawn a tolerant steady frame (`warmsVariants`: loading, the starting-state draw, the first presentation) what draws next is a strict frame, so such a preparation probes strictly: a tolerant one would find the swaps and answer the strict frames that follow "not ready" without probing again. A caller joins a preparation already running for the generation whatever its tolerance: a strict owner (loading, a quality step) that joins a tolerant one still sees a swap in its own `isReady()` and presentation checks, and steady frames that join an owner's strict preparation wait for it, as they wait for the new resources it prepares.
  - Everything else waits for every requested variant: loading presentations (`render(true)`), `isReady()` (loading owners and Play's end-of-frame scalability confirmation, which therefore confirms only once the new variants draw; a presented Play frame asks it only while `RuntimeScalability.awaitsPresentation` holds, for the first frame, a prepared transaction or settings applied without preparation, or while post-processing programs wait for release, so a steady frame runs no second strict round after its own readiness), previews and thumbnails. A validated frame of a view that draws steady frames (a frame report's) first probes as a steady frame does: such frames repeat until one draws, and only a tolerant round admits a swap's new variant, so otherwise a report requested during a swap held the view for good. A probe that tolerates no swap re-probes rather than trust a pass that tolerated one, except right after a tolerant probe (this frame or the last, no input changed) found a swap: then the answer is "not ready" without walking the scene again. The probe records its own tolerance (`collectingEffects`), since a preparation marks its probe after the tolerance scope closed.
  - A tolerated probe clears a submesh's `_areLightsDisposed` before asking it: Babylon otherwise refuses to draw a previous Effect after one of its lights was disposed, and the layout binding never binds a disposed light.
  - A new shadow map (a light starting to cast, a map moved to another light or resumed from standby) holds nothing either. A changed shadow set needs no graph preparation: `ManagedShadowsTask` borrows the controller's maps by reference, so a frame marks readiness dirty (`restaleChangedShadows`) and its probe binds the set as it is. A map awaiting its first draw (`awaitsFirstDraw`, [`shadow-first-draw.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/shadow-first-draw.ts)) samples neutrally (darkness 1, lit) and draws only once every caster's depth variant is ready, checked before its refresh is consumed, since a render-once map drawn without some casters would keep the gap. Meanwhile a tolerant probe requests those depth variants without letting them fail: the task's own map check, the scene probe's render targets, and `Mesh.isReady`'s caster check (`deferCasterChecks` answers ready for every map's generator while it asks one mesh) each count it as a swap, so steady frames re-probe until it draws. A scene view's probe asks each map its shadow task binds once, in that task (`ManagedShadowsTask.probesMap`); the scene probe (`isSceneFrameReady`'s `probedElsewhere`) asks only the others. A map that has drawn keeps drawing without a caster still compiling (a spawned or newly revealed one): Babylon skips that caster and resets the map's refresh, so the map redraws until it is ready. Receivers swap to their shadowed variants as for any light change; one that adopts before the map draws reads it as lit. No second graph exists, so nothing is held twice. Strict probes (loading, presentations) still wait for every caster.
  - Spawns (decision 3). Play's load compiles what it may spawn before the first frame (`warmPlaySpawnables` in `create-engine.ts`, [`spawnable-warm.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/spawnable-warm.ts)), after the Scene's own warm-up and against the same final lighting: every surface Material document its sources hold on a staged box (a primitive's attributes) and every model as a staged instance of its container (the Classes, Prefabs and spawnable actors of the required closure included). The containers start loading as the load's shader warm-up starts (`loadSpawnableContainers`), so their decoding overlaps it; staging waits for the warm-up, since staged meshes must not join it and one Material's compiles must not overlap (`forceCompilation` saves and restores its `allowShaderHotSwapping`). Staging is not skipped for a model or Material the Scene already places: a placed actor draws its slots' Materials with its own Receive Shadows and visibility, so its warmed variants do not cover a staged mesh's. A latent spawn's preload (Spawn Actor, a Class load: `acquireSceneSources` with `prepare` and `warm`, from both hosts' `assetPreload` handlers) compiles what it prepared the same way before it resolves; a scene's own source preparation leaves `warm` off, since its lighting is not final yet and its load warms afterwards. Staged meshes are unpickable receivers that never cast (a render-once map would keep them), invisible and disposed at once, except in a Play load (`keepForFirstDraw`), where a compiled program still holds iPad WebKit at its first draw: they are shown until the starting-state draw has drawn them behind the loading screen (they make it draw, and its `onDrawn` disposes them; the world's first presented frame releases any left, so they never show); the Effects they compiled stay in the Engine's cache, because Babylon's `forceCompilation` compiles on a temporary submesh it never disposes (which keeps its reference), so a later mesh with the same variant finds its Effect ready. Model actors already compile their own instance before it publishes (`prepareInstanceMaterials`). `spawnableWarmStats()` reports the load's warm-up. It is a cache pre-fill: a variant also depends on the mesh's own attributes (skinning, morph targets, instances) and lighting, so a miss is possible.
  - Starting-state draw. A Play-mode load (editor Play, Preview Build and the exported player) draws its starting state behind its loading screen, before the first presented frame, without view culling ([`starting-state-draw.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/starting-state-draw.ts), created by `prewarmSceneMaterials` after the world graph prepares; `skipFrustumClipping`): meshes outside the starting view have their program's first use there rather than when gameplay first shows them. The editor's world view (`editor`) draws a loaded Scene once the same way behind its loading dialog (`draws` always, settled at once): it culls by view too, and its load compiles every mesh's programs without drawing those outside the starting view, each of which would otherwise first draw when the camera first turns to it. It keeps the draw's Effects only until its first presented frame (`endStartingDraw` in `finishPresentation`), since an edited Scene's Materials come and go; a load without a loading dialog (not blocking) runs no shader warm-up and no such draw. The rest of this entry is Play's. Its frames also draw the particle systems that have not started (`ParticleService.drawingUnstartedSystems`): Play keeps them unstarted and paused until the load ends and then starts them all in one frame, so without this that frame would hold every particle program's first draw. Each draws there at zero speed, emitting nothing (a CPU system one particle, as it draws only live ones), and is reset, unstarted, after the frame, so gameplay starts, prewarms and emits it as before. The library's systems that no component plays, the ones a spawn may play, are prepared for it too (`ParticleService.prepareSpawnableSystems`: each on an emitter of its own, its diagnostics held until a component plays it), drawn the same way, and retired once it is done (`releaseSpawnableSystems`), their programs kept compiled until the next load or the session's end, so a spawn neither compiles nor first-draws them; the draw settles once they are ready (`spawnableSystemsPending`), and one that became ready after its first draw is drawn again (`spawnableSystemsUndrawn`, `drawAgain`), since no mesh holds its programs. A system whose programs never drew on the Engine (one no load knew of; `effectDrawnOn`) starts within a per-frame first-draw budget (`FIRST_DRAWS_PER_FRAME`, one system a frame), so several spawned in one frame start over successive frames. The outline task's masks draw every member and every strict occluder in its frames (`skipFrustumClipping` turns off the task's view cull), so a mesh coming into view later has drawn its mask variant. Manual Render Target captures not yet requested are rehearsed while it runs (`RenderTargetCaptures.rehearseManualCaptures`): each draws once into its output, unpublished (its consumers keep the fallback) and keeping its request state, so a data capture's override Materials compile and draw there rather than at the first request. A rehearsal draws only inside the view's frame, so while one is tried and not yet drawn (`rehearsalPending`) the starting state draws the view again (`drawAgain`, as for an undrawn Effect) rather than wait without drawing, which would hold it until its stall rule ended it. A frame counts as drawing only the Effects that were ready (or failed) as it drew; one still compiling draws in a later frame. It draws only when the Scene holds an authored light (`authoredLight:`), the load may spawn a light (`spawnableLights`), the spawn warm-up staged meshes for it, or a particle component is to play (`ParticleService.holdsPlayingComponents`); otherwise it draws no frame, since an extra loading frame steps GPU particles once more, which a paused Simulation's redraw shows, and that load's meshes outside the starting view first draw when gameplay shows them. The spawnable lights come from the owned sources' `spawnableLights` ([`spawnable-lights.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/spawnable-lights.ts), `spawnableLightsOfLoad` in the hosts' source preparations: editor Play's startup, session and Scene preparations, the player's startup and acquired sources): the Light and Hemispheric Fill Light components of the prefab templates of the Classes they hold, and the lights their scripts' Add Component calls add (`spawnableLightsFromScripts`: a named light class's defaults, an unshadowed point or a fill; every light class's when a pin or variable feeds the class), and, when the load may spawn a Class it does not hold (`spawnsUnseenClasses`), a generic point and spot light, each with and without shadows, in that Class's place (`UNSEEN_CLASS_LIGHTS`; a Spawn Actor's Class is taken never to carry a directional or hemispheric light). A Class the load does not hold is Load On Demand (a Class only a Spawn Actor names arrives with its spawn's preload), and the signal is the same in both hosts, erring toward yes: an asset of the load's catalog closure depends (softly) on a Class asset none of its compiled Classes belongs to (editor Play reads the registry's `dependenciesFor`, the player its manifest entries' `dependencies`), or a compiled Class's Spawn Actor (`ctx.spawnActorAsync`, the source text Spawn Actor compiles to) takes its Class from anything but a string naming a Class the load holds (a variable, a pin, an engine base Class). Frames draw while the meshes hold an Effect no frame of the draw drew (a frame counts as drawing the Effects the meshes held when it drew; its post-draw probe can request more, which the next ready frame draws); after the first, it waits, without drawing, until no Full Quality capture view that draw created is still preparing (`RenderTargetCaptures.preparing`, its `settled`; a loading frame never publishes a capture, and a view whose preparation failed, such as one the rendering budget refused, retries only from its own draw, so it counts as settled), so such a view builds its draw wrappers' defines in the starting state. These frames are drawn in `drawOwner` but never presented (the canvas is cleared, as for the loading frames before them), and each restarts the first-frame timeout. The frame that ends it without drawing (its last draw left nothing undrawn, or a budget ended it) asks readiness again, its staged meshes and spawnable systems gone, and presents rather than show one more cleared loading frame. The draw itself is not skipped on a repeat Play whose programs were all drawn before: without view culling it requests pass variants (geometry, depth pre-pass, outline masks, shadow casters) for meshes outside the starting view that no culled probe asked for, and creates capture views, so what it would draw is known only once it has. The Effects the meshes hold keep one extra reference until the draw is released (`release`, from `endStartingDraw`: the next world load's `sceneLoading`, a superseded or failed presentation) or the Scene is disposed. Play keeps one Scene across its loads, so a level's retained Effects must go with the next load. Budgets: a draw that has not drawn everything within `STARTING_STATE_DRAW_MS` (10 s) ends as it is, unless a shader finished meanwhile (compile progress renews its budget, on one `DriverCompileWatch`), and the whole draw ends `STARTING_STATE_CEILING_MS` (120 s) after its first frame, inside the first presentation's own compile watch (`SCENE_PREPARATION_CEILING_MS`, 300 s from `presentFirstFrame`), whose renewals every starting-state frame's restart relies on. While the world loads, `renderDiagnostics().worldLoad` reports the world's pending first frame (ready, draw attempts, rendered, GPU pending, copied); Play's **Copy Details** prints it as its `World load` line. `renderDiagnostics().textureMemory` estimates the GPU bytes of every texture the Engine holds ([`texture-memory-estimate.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/texture-memory-estimate.ts): layers, cube faces, mips, MSAA samples; compressed formats at half a byte or a byte per texel), which Copy Details prints with the host's real memory counters (`Memory` line) and the context-loss error names. The editor's world view and SceneLayers draw no starting state.
  - A mesh that has never drawn with its Material and has nothing to draw meanwhile (a spawn the warm-up missed, a synchronous spawn, a changed Material: some submesh without a ready Effect in the probed pass) is hidden instead of holding the view ([`reveal-pending.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/reveal-pending.ts)). A tolerant probe that finds its variant compiling marks it (`hideUntilReady`), counts it as a swap and passes; every pass then leaves it out (each Scene tracks its hidden meshes that are not disposed, so a cull over a Scene that hides none skips the scan, `hasRevealPending`): the camera cull's output (object passes and their depth pre-passes, geometry and spatial effects, the water split), shadow caster lists, outline masks and picking (`pickAtCanvas`, the GPU picker). Its visibility flags, gameplay and physics are untouched.
  - Reveal. The probes walk the cull's input, so every pass still probes a hidden mesh: each notes whether its own variant is ready (`noteRevealProbe`: colour, the transparent depth pre-pass, geometry buffers, and in the colour probe the caster depth variant for every shadow map that has drawn; a map awaiting its first draw waits for its casters itself). The forward graph's readiness round (`revealRound` around `ForwardSceneFrameGraph.isReady`) reveals at its end, when it passed: a hidden mesh no pass found unready is ready, and the hidden meshes of one actor (the nearest `actor-{slot}` or `editorActor:` root) are revealed together once each that can draw is ready, so an actor appears whole and the frame that follows draws it. A hidden mesh whose shader failed to compile stays hidden, reported once, and no longer holds back its actor. A mesh is never hidden again once it has drawn with its Material (`hasDrawn`, recorded by every probe that finds it ready and by its reveal): a later variant change hot-swaps it, and a pass variant it never had (a geometry pass added, a depth pre-pass once it blends) waits as before.
  - A reveal redraws the shadow maps the mesh casts into (`onMeshRevealed` in the shadow controller resets their refresh counters): a point or spot light's map only when the mesh lies within the light's range (`casterWithinShadowRange`, and for a spot light within the cone around its square shadow frustum's corners, `spotShadowCone`, one cone each call rewrites, tested conservatively), a sun's maps always. `Mesh.isReady` still asks a hidden mesh's caster depth variant for every drawn map among its `lightSources`, in range or not, so a reveal can wait for a variant it does not draw yet, never the reverse. A hidden mesh casts into no map.
  - First-draw budget (decision 5, `FIRST_DRAWS_PER_FRAME` in [`variant-first-draws.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/variant-first-draws.ts): 1, since each first draw holds the thread). Once a world view draws steady frames (`ForwardSceneFrameGraph.warmsVariants`, cleared by an invalidation), an Effect its readiness rounds first request (`withFirstDrawRound`; strict ones included: Play's end-of-frame question, while a scalability transaction awaits its frame, often asks first) is cold: it reports not ready in the view's main object pass and blended depth pre-pass (`registerFirstDrawPasses`), so Babylon's hot swap keeps the previous Effect and a spawn stays hidden; in other passes (the camera's probe pass, shadow maps, geometry passes) it answers with its own `isReady`. It is admissible once linked. A cold Effect turns ready only when admitted: by a steady frame's tolerant round, or by the steady frame itself (`admitSteadyFirstDraws`, called by the view before it draws, so a cold variant no probe counts as a swap, such as one a re-planned graph's new pass first asks for in its real draws, never waits for a round that does not come). Each Engine frame (`engine.frameId`: its steady frame, its rounds, a tolerant preparation and a second view share it) admits at most `FIRST_DRAWS_PER_FRAME`, oldest first; one that failed to link, every fallback included, leaves the queue without using the budget; one that links inside the frame that requests it (SwiftShader, which has no parallel compile) is admitted at once while the frame has room. A burst of swaps (a light switching off under many Materials) therefore adopts its new variants over several frames, each Material drawing its previous Effect meanwhile, so the first draws of new programs (about 100 ms each on iPad WebKit WebGL2) spread instead of landing in one frame. A strict question (`isReady()`, Play's end-of-frame check) sees an Effect it first requests as cold, so not ready, and the steady frames admit it. Since only tolerant rounds admit, a strict round of a view's new run (after an invalidation, until its next steady frame) probes a cold Effect as it is and settles it once ready; otherwise an Effect left cold by the previous run (a Material swapped just before an invalidation) held that run's preparation for good. A steady view's strict round (a preparation after the view drew, which draws no frame) admits from the same Engine-frame budget, so a question waiting on several linked cold Effects (a quality change's preparation) passes within as many frames instead of holding until a stall, and their first draws never land in one frame together. An Effect some draw on the Engine drew with (`effectDrawnOn`, the first-draw tracker's record) is never cold, and one compiled but never drawn is (a latent spawn preparation's warm-up, a mesh hidden at load), so its first use also keeps to the budget; on an Engine no view tracks, every Effect it held at installation or returned since counts as drawn.
  - First-draw stats ([`first-draw-stats.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/first-draw-stats.ts)): the measurement for a program's first draw, which no readiness probe sees. `trackFirstDraws` (every `createEngine` view, once per Engine) records each Effect the Engine draws (`drawElementsType` / `drawArraysType`, `_currentEffect`). A Play handle's session (`openFirstDrawStats`) counts the Effects first drawn between a world load's first presented frame (`finishPresentation`) and the next `sceneLoading`, keeping totals across loads: how many, how many programs the Engine held when counting last resumed (`loadPrograms`: what the load compiled), how many of the first drawn ones it held then (compiled by the load and not drawn by it: the spawn warm-up's, a model's detail levels, meshes hidden at load; the starting-state draw draws without view culling, so meshes outside the starting view are drawn), the frames that held them, those longer than `FIRST_DRAW_STALL_MS` (100 ms, begin to next begin, so a stall outside the main thread counts too), the worst and the most in one frame. It also keeps the frames it counted that took longer than 100 ms, with or without a first draw (`longFrames`, and the `LONG_FRAMES_KEPT` longest), since WebKit has no Long Tasks API: each frame's length, its time from the Engine's frame begin to its end (the rest ran outside the Engine's frame: scripts, layout, garbage collection, or waiting for the next animation frame), its first draws and the Effects created in it (`createEffect` returning an Effect the Engine has not seen; WebGL2 links it at its first use). Each first draw also counts to the pass that made it (`currentDrawPass`, the view's draw pass attribution; `other` outside an attributed frame), in the session's totals (`passes`) and in its long frame, so a report says whether late programs are shadow depth variants, the colour pass's or an outline's. `renderDiagnostics().firstDraws` carries it to `stat draws` and the Feature Test check report. The tracker also keeps the pass that first drew each Effect, so `programInventory` (`RenderDiagnostics.programs`) can say of the programs the Engine holds how many it never drew and which passes the others belong to. It also says what they are variants of (`programVariants`, computed again only when the set of programs changed): the shaders they compile (an Effect's vertex and fragment shader names: a Material's build, a pass's own shader), the most programs one shader has, and the defines that differ between programs of one shader, each cause with the shaders it splits (defines that split the same shaders are one cause, named by the shortest). Its `materials` is the Material compiler's census ([`material-program-census.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/material-program-census.ts)): the compiler's live builds (Materials, Instances, private copies, surface and water variants) against the distinct build ids among them, the builds that adopted an earlier build's program (`shareCompiledProgram`, `shareParticleProgram`) and the texture-arrival rebuilds whose shader text did not change (each keeps its program, `shareCompiledProgram`); `stat draws` shows it as `programs N · Materials B on P`. A Check also times every call on the context by what it does ([`webgl-call-timer.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/webgl-call-timer.ts), `EngineHandle.timeWebGlCalls`, `webGlCallTotals`): each method on the context's prototype chain becomes an own property timing the call it shadows (a wrapper already installed as an own property included), totals per group (`WEBGL_CALL_GROUPS`, by name: `webGlCallGroup`) and the Engine frames a view rendered in (`FrameBackpressure.renderedThisFrame` at the frame end); stopping puts back each own property it found or deletes its own, so off it costs nothing. It and the draw stub never run together (`create-engine.ts` refuses either while the other is on), and both end on a context loss and with the view. A Check also measures a frame without its draws ([`webgl-draw-stub.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/webgl-draw-stub.ts), `EngineHandle.stubWebGlDraws`): every draw, clear, framebuffer blit and uniform upload on the context becomes a no-op (own properties shadowing the context's methods) except `uniform1i`, `uniform1iv` and `uniformBlockBinding`, which set up a program linked meanwhile; whatever binds, allocates or uploads stays real, so the context stays as the Engine believes it is, and ending it forgets each program's cached uniform values. A view's disposal and a context loss end it. A program's identity is meant to follow its Material, its pass and its vertex layout only, with light kind, environment and the like bound as values; `e2e/feature-test-smoke.spec.ts` asserts on Feature Test main in Play that no define already turned into a bound value splits a shader (a local light's class and distance output, the environment's presence and form), so a change that makes one compile programs again fails there; a slice that binds another adds its define to that list.
  - Still blocking: the first draw of a new program on iPad WebKit WebGL2 blocks its thread, for a variant the load did not draw or warm; the first-draw budget spreads those draws over frames.
  - Acceptance is CI only: [`compile-hold-acceptance.test.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/compile-hold-acceptance.test.ts) runs one Play scenario per cause under injected driver models (synchronous links, a parallel-compile driver whose new Effects stay pending), and the lighting CI gate there (a scripted run of light changes compiles nothing), and [`e2e/variant-swap.spec.ts`](../../../e2e/variant-swap.spec.ts) draws previous Effects on real WebGL2 (SwiftShader) with their new variants (fog switched on) held back and fails on any GL error. In the harness a Text3D, a box, a box with a 2D Text part and a sprite re-sent unchanged every tick keep their meshes and draw every frame under both models, and a Text3D whose text changes every tick draws each new mesh in its first frame with nothing compiled. In [`light-neutral-tasks.test.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/light-neutral-tasks.test.ts) a real geometry pass keeps a PBR or Standard surface's light defines across a light change, but not a Standard surface's in a reflectivity pass, an unregistered pass's, or across a change to the Material's lighting. A shadowed spot whose map waits on standby enters the Forward budget with no frame held (its returning map's casters' depth variants share the first-draw budget, so its receivers adopt one frame later). A spot, a point and the sun starting to cast shadows compile no Effect and hold no frame under both models: the map draws in the change's frame, since its casters' depth variants were compiled at load (`warmCasterVariants`). In [`shadow-controller.test.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/shadow-controller.test.ts) the maps that hold those variants (`ShadowCasterVariants`) are built only into room the real maps leave, and go when a sync needs that room for a real map, with new shadow settings, a restored context and the Scene's disposal. On real WebGL2 the same admissions, the first spot map's included, link no program. Jumping straight in, they compile then, and a new or returning map waits, neutral, for its casters' depth variants: no frame is held. It also adopts a burst of six swaps at most `FIRST_DRAWS_PER_FRAME` per frame, and a point switching off and a spot starting to cast shadows compile no Effect. Under the synchronous model, a shadowed spot a Class may spawn (`spawnableLights` from its prefab template, as editor Play passes them), spawning near the camera, also into a full Forward budget where it takes a point's place, a shadowed point spawning near the casters below a camera high above them (beyond its template's range), and a point and a shadowed spot of a Class the load does not hold (a dependency on a Class asset none of its scripts is), spawning near the camera, ask for no Effect for anything but the spawned actor's own meshes; with that Class held, a spot starting to cast shadows compiles none either. [`play-spawnable-lights.test.ts`](../../../apps/editor/src/services/play-spawnable-lights.test.ts) checks the signal on real compiled Spawn Actor calls (a named Class held or not, a Class from a variable) and on dependencies; on real WebGL2 the same changes, and a shadowed point of a kind no authored light has spawning near the camera (built by `createPlayLight`), link no program (`gl.linkProgram`, until the new map has drawn). No device measurement backs it.
- Compile hold: strict readiness presents only when ready, so a wait the cases above do not cover (a strict presentation, a graph still preparing or probing its passes' variants) leaves the canvas on its last image while the driver links the new effects. A `compileHolds` view (the world view's coordinator in `createEngine`: editor, Play, exported player; never an auxiliary one) reports that wait through `holdingForCompiles`. In Play a hold that has lasted `COMPILE_HOLD_PAUSE_DELAY_MS` (250 ms; a shorter hold pauses nothing and the release is immediate) pauses the game's time like a blocking load: the Engine pauses its own game time, audio and particles while it keeps drawing (`onCompileHold`), and the host adds the runtime pause reason `rendering` (editor Play and Preview Build through the session boundary; the exported player through its combined `setPaused`). Ticks, physics, scripts, timers and tweens stop; resuming resets the tick accumulator and discards the held interval, so nothing catches up. `rendering` is a time-only pause: hosts do not suppress or neutralize input for it, nothing exits pointer lock, and the runtime keeps queueing input (a held key stays held, a release during the hold lands); any other reason still resets and refuses input, also on top of a hold. The exported player's combined `setPaused` carries `reason: "rendering"` for it. A `rendering`-only pause never pauses the render scheduler or the view's game time from the host side: the frame that finds the effects ready must draw to end the hold.
  - It starts at a frame not drawn because the scene probe failed, or the graph is still preparing or probing its passes' variants, while one of the view's own effects is pending on the driver: the Effects its readiness probes requested through `createEffect` (`requestedEffects` of the forward graph, filled by [`effect-requests.ts`](../../../packages/render/src/effect-requests.ts) inside `isReady`, `isOutlineReady` and the scene probe), so a preview's, thumbnail's or Custom GLSL probe's compile on the shared Engine never counts. Effects built outside those probes (a task's, while the graph is built) are not attributed. It then lasts until a frame draws, even after the effects finished, so the game does not resume under a frozen picture. A wait that compiles nothing from the start holds the image only: a graph rebuilt for a resize or a resolution step, a texture still decoding, and a link that completes inside the call (SwiftShader, which has no parallel compile) leave nothing pending. iPad WebKit WebGL2 keeps new effects pending while they link, so it holds; a draw's first use of a new program still blocks its thread, which no hold covers.
